or service that actually starts Maya (for systemd, the user manager's hard limit matters as well as the service's
`LimitMEMLOCK`). An unlimited limit does not supply RAM. A failed `mlock`, invalid range or unsupported table type
stops initialization with the reason; the engine does not silently fall back to disk.

**NUMA and cache pressure.** Locked file mappings can reuse pages already on one socket even with an interleave
policy. On a multi-socket host inspect `numastat -p PID`, `/proc/PID/numa_maps` and each node's free memory. Leave
headroom on every node. Unused tmpfs copies of another model consume RAM and possibly swap; they are not ordinary
reclaimable disk cache. These settings do not alter global cache limits, delete files or move pages between nodes.
Do not truncate or replace a mapped weight file while its model is running.

**Verify the result.** Look for `Engram RAM ONLY` and the count of experts copied to RAM in the engine log.
On Linux, check that the relevant table ranges in `/proc/PID/smaps` have `VmFlags: ... lo`, `Rss == Size`, and
`Swap: 0`; check the whole process's `VmSwap` separately. The expert-copy count must equal the CPU-lane expert
count. GPU-resident experts are separate. Do not infer these guarantees from total machine `MemAvailable` alone.

A small model-free regression test is available as `python tools/test_ds4_engram_ram.py` on Linux with a C++ compiler.
It checks file/RAM byte agreement, reads after unlink, split FP8 scales, MXFP8 extraction, bounds, and failure when
the memlock limit is zero. Run it as an ordinary user without `CAP_IPC_LOCK`.

## DeepSeek: the CPU lane's threads

DeepSeek's engine computes the routed experts that are not in VRAM on the CPU (the CPU lane), a thread per physical
core; a core's second thread (SMT) is left to the disk and Engram readers. Where the PC offers another way - the
performance cores alone on a hybrid CPU (Intel's P- and E-cores), the first socket's cores alone on two sockets or
more (the experts read in place) - a start times each, once the experts are placed, on the same calls a decode step
makes (several rounds, in turns), and keeps another way only when it is more than 5% faster. The choice never changes
an answer, only the speed; the log says what was measured (`ds4: CPU lane: node, 22 threads (measured, ...)`). It
needs at least 8 experts of one layer on the lane (otherwise the lane hardly runs). Only the CPUs the engine may run
on are used (`taskset`, a container's cpuset).

| Setting | Default | |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `--cpu-threads N` (the config's engine `args`) | a thread per core | at most N threads, a thread per core; nothing is measured |
| `MAYA_DS4_CPU_LANE` | measured | `cores`, `pcores`, `node` or `smt` (a thread per logical CPU): that way, nothing is measured |
| `DS4_COPY_NUMA` (the config's `"env"`) | 2 parts at most | the CPU lane's RAM copies of its experts in this many NUMA parts, `1`-`4`, each on its own node with a worker pool there - so a host of three or four sockets uses them all (the default takes two). 4x Xeon E7-8880 v4 (88 cores), Maya-S + DSpark, `--mmap-experts off`: two RTX 3090s (a pair on sockets 0-1, and one on 2-3, two runs each on the first) 37-39 -> 49 tok/s decode, after a 68K prompt 24-26 -> 32-33 tok/s, the prompt read 644-655 -> 805 tok/s on the first pair (802 both ways on the second); four RTX 3090s (less on the CPU lane) unchanged |

## DeepSeek: IQ1_S experts on AMD Zen, Zen+ and Zen 2 CPUs

ggml-cpu builds IQ1_S's grid indices (Maya-XS's experts) with the BMI2 `pdep` instruction. AMD's family 17h CPUs
(Zen, Zen+, Zen 2: Ryzen 1000 / 2000 / 3000 desktop, Ryzen 4000 and a few 5000 laptop chips, EPYC 7001 / 7002,
Threadripper 1000-3000) and Hygon's
run `pdep` in microcode, hundreds of cycles each, so the CPU lane spent most of an expert there. On those CPUs the
CPU lane now takes its own IQ1_S rows, which build the indices with plain shifts: the same numbers bit for bit
(checked against ggml-cpu's dot at the start; if they ever differ, ggml-cpu's rows are kept). A user's report from
an EPYC (Zen 2) with Maya-XS: an expert on the CPU 1.6 -> 0.44 ms. Zen 3 and later and Intel CPUs keep the
measured choice as before. The log says `ds4: CPU lane IQ1_S rows: ours without pdep (...)` when it applies.

| Setting | Default | |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `DS4_IQ1_PDEP` | `0` on AMD family 17h / Hygon, else `1` | `0`: the rows without `pdep` on any CPU (tests: `ds4_iq1_rows_test` checks them against ggml-cpu); `1`: the measured choice (ggml-cpu's dot for a single token) |

## DeepSeek: a GEMV call's int8 inputs rounded in one launch

A GGUF pack's Q8_0 (and low-bit dense) GEMVs round their activations to int8 first, one small launch per job: a
decoded token runs a few hundred of them per GPU, each about 2 us of work behind a launch gap. With
`MAYA_DS4_Q8_BATCH=1` a call's jobs are rounded in one launch before its GEMVs, by the same per-block code, so the
int8 values, the scales and the answers are bit for bit the same. `MAYA_DS4_Q8_CHECK=1` checks the batched rounding
against the per-job one on every GPU at load (any difference fails the start).

| Setting | Default | |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `MAYA_DS4_Q8_BATCH` | off | `1`: a GEMV call's int8 rounding in one launch (the same bits) |
| `MAYA_DS4_Q8_CHECK` | off | `1`: the batched rounding checked against the per-job one on every GPU at load |

## DeepSeek: tensor parallel 2 on Maya's own quants

`--tp n` splits each expert's FFN between the group's GPUs, and a GGUF's experts split only in whole blocks of their
type: Maya's quants use 256-value blocks, n_ff 2304 = 9 blocks, so `--tp 2` (or 4 or 8) falls back to 1 with
"tensor parallel 2 -> 1". `MAYA_DS4_TP_GGUF_UNEVEN=1` lets a member take whole blocks as even as they go, 5 + 4 for
two GPUs, with the odd block going round the members layer by layer, as EXL3's split128 does. Each member loads,
streams and swaps its own range, the shared expert is split at the same range, and each member's exact expert bytes
are fitted to its own VRAM (a slot fewer at a time when the mean overshoots). With `DS4_FLOW_TP=1` its members take
the prompt flow too, each streaming its own rows, the down gathered on the GPU (on a disk-streaming box the group's
prompts are much slower off the flow).
`MAYA_DS4_TP_CHECK=1` checks every expert format and rotation at load, before any request: one token through each
member's parts as decode runs them, summed on the host and compared with the whole expert and shared expert (more than
1e-3 of the largest output fails the start).

| Setting | Default | |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `MAYA_DS4_TP_GGUF_UNEVEN` | off | `1`: a GGUF's experts split in whole blocks, as even as they go, so its group can form |
| `MAYA_DS4_TP_CHECK` | off | `1`: each member's parts checked against the whole expert at load |

## DeepSeek: the DSpark drafter's blocks on a group's members

The drafter's blocks run whole on the drafter's group's lead, and their VRAM experts come out of the lead's room. A
tensor-parallel group's members hold the same trunk slots, so every drafter byte on the lead leaves as many idle on
the other members. `MAYA_DS4_DRAFT_DEV` puts each block on a member of its own: its weights, ring and VRAM experts on
that GPU, out of that member's room (the 99% rule as before, the extra experts only while the member keeps the next
tightest member's room), so the tightest member has more left for the trunk. Each block's GPU projects the taps
itself; between two blocks on different GPUs their rows go through pinned host memory, as a layer split's groups
hand theirs. The load line names the blocks a GPU holds (`+ blocks 0`).

| Setting | Default | |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `MAYA_DS4_DRAFT_DEV` | off (every block on the lead) | the member each DSpark block runs on: one number for every block, or one a block (`1,0,0`: block 0 on member 1, the others on the lead); 0 is the lead |

## DeepSeek: a helper GPU for the experts VRAM leaves over

A GGUF pack's routed experts that do not fit the layer GPUs' VRAM run on the CPU lane. A GPU that is not one of the
layer devices - an integrated one (Strix Halo's Radeon 8060S beside discrete cards) or a small extra card - can take
those instead: `MAYA_DS4_HELPER=<visible GPU>` with `MAYA_DS4_HELPER_GB=<GB>` gives it a pool of the most-routed
experts the layer GPUs do not hold (by the expert profile). Its picks run on its own stream from the window's plan
(decode and a DSpark verify window), the CPU lane's service thread adds the sums, and a failure or a 10 s silence hands
the picks back to the CPU lane. An expert is summed on the helper instead of the CPU lane, so its output can differ in
the last bits. The DeepSeek counterpart of GLM's `MAYA_GLM_EXPERT_HELPER` (#201). **Linux only (tested); Windows +
AMD untested** - there AMD's driver starts queued GPU work only when the host waits, so the helper is likely to
stay silent past the 10 s limit and hand its picks back to the CPU lane.

| Setting | Default | |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `MAYA_DS4_HELPER` | off | the helper's visible GPU index (not one of `--gpus`); with `MAYA_DS4_HELPER_GB`. Linux only (tested); Windows + AMD untested |
| `MAYA_DS4_HELPER_GB` | - | its experts' pool, GB |
| `MAYA_DS4_WARM_CAP_GB` | - | required with the helper: the load's experts in host RAM, GB, beside the pool (an integrated GPU's pool is host RAM too); ignored under `DS4_REQUIRE_ALL_RAM=1` (every expert in RAM) |
| `MAYA_DS4_HELPER_CHECK` | off | `1`: at load, two tokens per expert format through the helper against the CPU lane, and its grouped window path against its per-token one, bit for bit |

## DeepSeek: where the measured hipBLASLt picks are kept (AMD)

On AMD the engine measures hipBLASLt's kernels for each prompt GEMM shape at its first use on a GPU and keeps the
picks in `<pack>/lt-picks.txt`, so later starts reuse them. A pack whose files must not change (one shared between
installs, or checked by hash before each start) can keep them elsewhere.

| Setting | Default | |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `MAYA_DS4_LT_STORE` | `<pack>/lt-picks.txt` | the picks in this file instead; empty: none kept, each start measures its own |

## DeepSeek: the starting expert profile

The engine keeps how often each expert was routed in `<pack>/expert-profile.bin`: at a start the most-routed experts
go into VRAM and are read ahead into RAM first, and the counts grow with every answer (saved after each one). A new
pack has no counts yet, so the setup copies `data/expert-profile-ds4.bin` into it - DeepSeek's routing over a mix of
chat, code, multilingual text, reasoning and tool calls (`tools/ds4/profile.py`). Its counts weigh as much as about
2,000 tokens of answers, so what your PC routes soon outweighs them. A pack that already has a profile keeps its own;
an `--expert-profile` in the config's `args` (a file, or `none`) is used instead.

## DeepSeek: conversations kept (an agent's parent and workers on one model)

The engine holds one conversation: a prompt that continues it reuses its state, anything else starts over. An agent
whose parent and workers interleave - and whose prompts alternate between ~70k and ~185k tokens - then reads the
whole prompt again on every switch (257 of 632 requests in one log read from token 0; the 142 of them longer than 30k
tokens read at ~930 tok/s: a 70k-token prompt takes ~75 s, a 185k one ~200 s). With this on, the conversation in the
model is set aside when another one takes its place - its whole state: the position, every layer's 128-token window
ring, the compressors' partial groups and the four KV sources' compressed KV and index keys up to it - and a request
that continues a kept conversation takes it back, reading only its new tokens (a 12k-token conversation back in ~0.1 s
instead of ~12 s). A conversation with pictures is kept too: it holds each picture's place and fingerprint (not
the pixels), and comes back only for a prompt with the same pictures at the same places. Off by default, so the
engine is unchanged unless asked.

| Setting | Default | |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `MAYA_DS4_SLOT_KEEP` | off | `1`: keep the conversation when another one takes the model, and across runs in the folder; `0`: every prompt that does not continue the conversation in the model reads it from token 0, as before |
| `MAYA_DS4_KEEP_THINKING` | off | `1`: an earlier turn's reasoning (`reasoning_content`) stays in the prompt, where DeepSeek's own serving drops it at the next user turn. A tool-less chat needs this as well as `MAYA_DS4_SLOT_KEEP`: with thinking dropped, every turn renders a history that differs at each assistant turn, so its next turn continues no state at all (a Strix Halo, Ryzen AI Max: a 4,215-token follow-up behind a 4,197-token state read in 19.3 s; with both on, it reused 4,197 of 4,215 and read in 2.7 s). Requests that carry tools keep their thinking already (`serve/ds41_encoding.py`), so agent traffic is unchanged. The prompts are longer by the reasoning they carry - paid once as reuse, not as a read |
| `MAYA_DS4_SLOT_CAP` | 4 | the conversations kept in RAM (one is tens to hundreds of MB, the context's compressed KV being the most); past it the least recently used is written to the folder - RAM first, the folder past the cap - and read back from there (reading a 185k-token state off an NVMe: ~0.5 s, against ~200 s of re-reading); `0` = no conversations kept |
| `MAYA_DS4_SLOT_RAM_GB` | 0 | optional steady-state RAM byte budget for parked conversations (GB): the incoming state is serialized before eviction, so its allocation can temporarily exceed the cap, then the least recently used spills to the folder; one RAM conversation is kept even when it alone is over the cap; `0` = count-limited only; invalid/non-finite/negative values fall back to `0` with a diagnostic |
| `MAYA_DS4_SLOT_GB` | 16 | the folder's conversations' total size (GB, over their `.bin` files): at start, and after every conversation written to it, the least recently used in the folder is removed while they are over it - never the one just written, so a conversation bigger than the cap stays |
| `MAYA_DS4_SLOT_DIR` | `<pack>/slots` | the folder: a conversation is a `.bin` (its state) and a `.meta` (this model's identity, its tokens and its size), both written under a temporary name first |
| `MAYA_DS4_SLOT_MIN` | 1024 | the shortest conversation set aside (tokens): a shorter one is read again in about the time its slot would take |
| `MAYA_DS4_SLOT_DAYS` | 7 | a slot not used for this many days is removed at start; so is any file not this model's, another engine's, or incomplete. The engine's end (QUIT or the end of its input) writes the conversations in RAM to the folder, so a restart reads on from them (the server gives it `MAYA_ENGINE_QUIT_S`, 50 s, to do so) |

The client sends each assistant turn's `reasoning_content` back with the turn, as it got it: from `content` alone the
prompt differs from the state and nothing is reused - no error and no log line.

The engine's log says what happened (`ds4 slots: saved/took back/wrote ...`; the server shows those lines as
`[maya] sessions: ...`). A slot's identity is the engine's version, the model's geometry and files and whether the
DSpark blocks are loaded, so a slot from another model, another pack or an engine that changed the state's layout is
refused rather than read.

## MiniMax-M3.1-Flash

`./maya.sh --setup --model MiniMax-M3.1-Flash` writes `maya-mm3.json` (Maya's GGUF quants: their own, below) with what fits this PC: the context (below), the
model's pictures on, the tensor-parallel groups from the GPU count, and the DSpark drafter only where this PC's speed
calibration (or your answer at the setup) says drafting is faster. A setup again keeps every engine option listed
here that the config already has.

**Defaults from MiniMax's model card**: temperature 1.0, top-p 0.95 (no top-k, no min-p). The thinking level a
request gets when it names none is `auto`: the card names no default effort, so none is set and the model decides
whether to think, as MiniMax's template does by default. The levels:

| `reasoning_effort` | The prompt |
|---|---|
| `auto` (default) | MiniMax's template as it is: adaptive thinking, no effort named |
| `low`, `medium`, `high`, `xhigh`, `max` | the same, with that effort (the card's five); the model still decides whether to think |
| `none` | thinking off: the turn opens with `</mm:think>`, the system prompt says thinking is disabled (MiniMax-M3's words) |

The prompt is Maya's own copy of MiniMax's template (`tools/mm3/chat_template.jinja`, used by every MiniMax pack):
byte for byte MiniMax's for every level but `none`. Change the default with `"reasoning_effort"` in the config.

**The context.** The setup offers 32K to 1M (the model's own reach) and recommends the largest, up to 256K, whose KV
cache fits beside the model in this PC's VRAM + RAM. With KV streaming (on by default) every position's K / V sits in
pinned RAM - about 120 KB a token in BF16, 62 KB in INT8 - and the newest 32K positions in VRAM as well. Past 256K the
engine has not been run end to end yet; the setup and the dashboard say so. The dashboard's Context size shows the
cache's RAM at each size and warns when it takes more than is free.

Engine options, in the config's `"args"`:

| Option | Default | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| `--max-context` | the setup's choice | the context, in tokens (the dashboard's Context size changes it) |
| `--kv-streaming` | `on` (the setup) | `on`: every position's K / V in pinned RAM, a ring of the newest in VRAM; `off`: all of it in VRAM |
| `--kv-resident` | 32768 | with KV streaming: the positions of each layer kept in VRAM |
| `--kv-cache` | `int8` | the K / V cache's format: `int8` or `fp8` (half of BF16's memory), `fp4` (a quarter), `bf16` (exact), or K and V apart (`int8,fp4`); the index keys stay BF16 |
| `--kv-rot` | `on` | the cache's rows rotated before they are rounded (outliers spread over the row); `--kv-rotate` is the same option |
| `--vision` | `on` (the setup) | the model's vision tower in the engine (~1.9 GB on its GPU); `off` frees it for experts (`--no-vision` at the setup) |
| `--vision-gpu` | 0 | which of the config's GPUs holds the vision tower |
| `--dspark` | `off` until measured | `on`: the DSpark drafter proposes tokens, the model checks them (the same tokens); `./maya.sh --speed-calibration` measures which is faster here |
| `--dspark-conf` | 0.7 | a draft is verified only while its confidence is at least this |
| `--tp` | from the GPU count | tensor parallel: the GPUs in groups of 4 or 2 (the first alone when the others make whole groups); `0`: layers split across the GPUs |
| `--cache-gb` | 1.5 | with `--tp`: each GPU's expert cache, out of its experts in VRAM - at least this; where the GPU memory is tight the engine makes it larger by itself (a NOTE), and when `--tp` does not fit at all it runs the layer split instead (a NOTE) |
| `--tp-chunk` | 1024 | with `--tp`: the prompt's tokens a pipeline chunk takes |
| `--tp-ffn-rows` | 0 | with `--tp`: a chunk's FFN in slices of this many tokens (0: the whole chunk; 256 keeps more experts in VRAM) |
| `--dspark-cost` | 0 | (> 0) drafts are verified only while their confidences' product stays above this |
| `--cpu-threads` | one a physical core | the CPU lane's workers (the experts VRAM does not hold are computed on the CPU from pinned RAM); `-1`: no CPU lane |
| `--reserve-gb` | 1.0 (0.3 with `--tp`) | VRAM each GPU keeps free beyond the plan |
| `--resident-cap` | as many as fit | routed experts kept in VRAM, a layer at most |
| `--chunk` | the prefill calibration's (1024 before it) | prompt tokens a forward takes at most; given here, the calibration's is not taken |
| `--prefill-gemm` | the prefill calibration's (`blas` before it) | Maya's GGUF quants: the prompt's dense matrix products through the BLAS (cuBLAS / hipBLAS) or, on AMD, `hipblaslt` (each shape's fastest kernel timed on this GPU once, kept beside the model in `lt-picks.txt`); `auto`: the calibration's |
| `--no-prefill-tune` | - | the engine's defaults instead of this PC's prefill calibration (`--prefill-tune FILE`: another file than the pack's `prefill-tune.txt`) |
| `--staging` | 48 | expert staging slots a GPU |
| `--profile` | `data/expert-profile-mm3.bin` | the routing profile: the most-routed experts are placed in VRAM |

**Maya's GGUF quants.** `./maya.sh --setup --model minimax-maya-xs` (or `minimax-maya-s`; before:
`--model MiniMax-M3.1-Flash --mm3-quant gguf-xs`, which still works) sets up Project Maya's own quants instead of the
EXL3 one: MiniMax M3.1 · Maya-XS (the experts in about 1.6 bits, ~93 GB: the whole model in a 128 GB PC's memory) or
MiniMax M3.1 · Maya-S (about 2.1 bits, ~122 GB), from
[peasantsmith/MiniMax-M3.1-Flash-Maya-GGUF](https://huggingface.co/peasantsmith/MiniMax-M3.1-Flash-Maya-GGUF)
(`Maya-XS/MiniMax-M3.1-Flash-Maya-XS-IQ1_S-0000N-of-00002.gguf`, `Maya-S/MiniMax-M3.1-Flash-Maya-S-IQ2_XXS-...`); each
gets a config of its own, `maya-minimax-maya-xs.json` / `maya-minimax-maya-s.json`. They run on NVIDIA
from V100 on and on AMD, Linux and Windows. The files follow llama.cpp's MiniMax-M3 layout; the sparse-attention
indexers of the first three layers sit in a small file of their own beside them (`...-maya-indexers.gguf`), which
Maya loads so the model runs as MiniMax defines it - keep it with the other files. Pictures and the DSpark drafter
come from the EXL3 repo's own files (its vision tower and its draft, about 4 GB, downloaded into
`MiniMax-M3.1-Flash-companions` beside the model, or taken from an EXL3 download already there), on NVIDIA and on
AMD alike. The options above apply, but for `--tp` (the GGUFs run the layer split) and `--cache-gb`;
`tools/mm3/pack_gguf.py --model <folder>` builds the pack by hand (`--text-only`: without the companions).

**The prefill calibration.** How a PC reads prompts fastest depends on its GPU, so it is measured there: the setup
(step 9), `./maya.sh --calibrate` and the first start of a MiniMax config set up before it run the engine once over a
~4K-token text (`maya-mm3 --calibrate-prefill`, tools/calibrate_mm3_prefill.py) with a few settings - the chunk of
tokens a step takes (larger ones only where every expert still fits in VRAM) and, with Maya's GGUF quants on AMD, the
matrix products' library - and keep the fastest. The engine stores it beside the model (`prefill-tune.txt`, a line
for this PC: its GPUs, their architecture, the GPU runtime and the driver) and takes it at every start; a new GPU or
driver gets the defaults until it is measured again. The config records the result (`installer.prefill_calibration`).
It takes one start of the model and a few minutes; the answers are the model's either way.

Setup and debugging variables:

| Variable | Default | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| `MAYA_MM3_HOST_FREE` | `1` | decode: a layer whose routed experts are all in VRAM plans them on the GPU, so a token or a verify window runs without waiting for the host at each layer; `0`: the host plans every layer (the same tokens) |
| `MAYA_MM3_GRAPH` | `1` | decode on one GPU with every MoE layer host-free: a token's and a verify window's forward replayed as one graph (the same tokens); `0`: launched kernel by kernel |
| `MAYA_MM3_WIN_SUBMIT` | `1` | Windows + AMD: each layer's queued work started as it is enqueued (the driver otherwise waits for the host); `0`: off |
| `MAYA_MM3_ATTN_WIDE`, `MAYA_MM3_MSA_WIDE` | `1` | the sparse attention's decode kernel with more threads, the indexer's prompt scores in register tiles - each the same results as before; `0`: the earlier kernels |
| `MAYA_MM3_LT_STORE` | the pack's `lt-picks.txt` | where hipBLASLt's measured kernels are kept |
| `MAYA_MM3_PROF` | off | debugging: `1` each forward's GPU time by part (events), `2` the same with the host waiting at each part (AMD), and the DSpark proposals' parts |
| `MAYA_MM3_MIN_CC` | - | testing only: `70` lets the setup take GPUs before RTX 30 / A100 (a V100), with a warning |
| `MAYA_MM3_IQ_Q8K` | `1` | GGUF quants: the routed experts' inputs rounded as the CPU lane rounds them (a scale every 256 values), so an expert gives the same sums on the GPU and the CPU; `0`: a scale every 32 |
| `MM3_TIMING`, `MM3_CONF`, `MM3_LANE_PROF`, `MM3_MISS_STATS`, `MM3_TP_PROF`, `MM3_TP_TRACE`, `MM3_NSYS` | off | debugging: the engine prints timings, the drafts' confidences, the CPU lane's and tensor parallel's profiles |
| `MM3_PROMOTE` | - | debugging: the expert cache's promotion rule with `--tp` (`threshold,pending,decay`) |
| `MM3_STRESS`, `MM3_STRESS_T` | off | debugging: a drafting stress run |
| `MM3_DUMP_KEPT`, `MM3_DUMP_HS` | off | debugging: write the sparse attention's kept blocks / hidden states to files |
| `MM3_TOPK` | the model's | debugging only: routes each token to this many experts - the answers are no longer the model's |


# Setting up Maya

The setup in detail: what its first run does, starting again and updating, and its options. Back to the
[README](../README.md).

## What the first run does

It first asks which model to set up:

- **GLM-5.3-Flash** - Project Maya's GGUF quants (or turboderp's EXL3 quants), from one GPU up; the steps below;
- **DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017** (experimental) - [LibertAIDAI's EXL3 quant at 2.9 bits](https://huggingface.co/LibertAIDAI/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017-EXL3-2.9bpw)
  (211 GB) or [at 3.5 bits](https://huggingface.co/LibertAIDAI/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017-EXL3-3.5bpw) (258 GB, about
  280 GB of VRAM and RAM; `--ds4-quant exl3-3.5`, its own folder; a second quant hard-links the Engram tables from the
  first instead of downloading them) and its two Engram tables from [DeepSeek's repo](https://huggingface.co/deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017)
  (203 GB), on its own engine (`build/maya-ds4`): Linux, NVIDIA V100 or newer (pictures: RTX 30 / A100 or newer); AMD experimental. It is fast with about 230 GB
  of VRAM and RAM together; with less, the setup warns and the engine reads the experts that do not fit from the
  model's files on the SSD (`--mmap-experts auto`), much slower - one RTX 3090 with 64 GB of RAM: prompts about 50
  tokens/s, answers 4-5 tokens/s (faster with `--dspark none`). The same steps with these differences: the
  contexts are 32K-1M (256K recommended where the PC's VRAM + RAM hold the model's files, else 64K; 512K and 1M
  take VRAM from the expert cache, and a prompt that long reads
  slowly - the indexer's work grows with the square of its length); step 5 shows both
  sources and checks every file against its pinned size (and, once downloaded, its sha256: `tools/ds4/download.json`, `download-3.5bpw.json`);
  `--model-dir DIR` uses files you already have; the pack is `DIR/maya-ds4/` (`tools/ds4/pack.py`, about a minute);
  pictures need nothing more - the model's own vision tower runs in the engine; the config is `maya-ds4.json`, with
  tensor parallel across pairs of equal GPUs (`--tp 2`). The engine balances its own layer split; `./maya.sh --calibrate`
  (not offered by the setup) measures each `--tp` that groups your GPUs differently and fewer CPU threads, an engine
  start each (about an hour on 9 GPUs), and keeps the fastest answers whose long prompts still read at 60% of the
  config's own `--tp`'s speed; a setting the engine does not start with is left out (`tools/calibrate_ds4_tp.py`; the
  result goes into `maya-ds4.json` and a setup again keeps it).

  Or from [unsloth's GGUFs](https://huggingface.co/unsloth/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017-GGUF) (`--ds4-quant`; the setup asks):
  **UD-Q8_K_XL** (508 GB, the default: dense weights BF16, routed experts MXFP4, the Engram tables FP8 - DeepSeek's own
  formats) or **UD-Q4_K_XL** (501 GB: dense weights Q8_0, less VRAM). The same engine reads them in place (the pack is
  `tools/ds4/pack_gguf.py`'s), from NVIDIA Volta (V100) on: the most-used experts in VRAM, the others read from the
  GGUF through the OS page cache - from RAM where they fit, from the SSD otherwise - so it runs whatever the memory
  (more VRAM and RAM is faster; keep the files on an NVMe SSD; `--mmap-experts off` copies as many of them into RAM as
  fit instead, on huge pages split across the CPU sockets' memory - measured no faster than the page cache). Prompts
  go through in chunks of 8192 tokens, also where tensor parallel falls back to one GPU per layer group (the log says
  `tensor parallel 2 -> 1`, as with the Maya quants) - before v1.1.17 those ran every prompt in windows of 16 tokens,
  an order of magnitude slower on long prompts (a community measurement, 7x RTX 3090, Maya-S read in place: 32K tokens
  99 tokens/s in windows, 1010 in chunks). The GGUFs have no DSpark drafter and no vision tower.
- **MiniMax-M3.1-Flash** (experimental; `--model MiniMax-M3.1-Flash`) - [Mia-AiLab's EXL3 quant at 3.5 bits](https://huggingface.co/Mia-AiLab/MiniMax-M3.1-Flash-EXL3-EAQ-v1-3.5-bpw)
  (197 GB with MiniMax's DSpark drafter; every file pinned and checked, `tools/mm3/download.json`) on its own engine
  (`build/maya-mm3`): NVIDIA from V100 on, Linux and Windows; AMD experimental (as GLM's EXL3 quants there: the
  kernels pass their tests on AMD, the whole model has not run on one yet) - on AMD, Maya's GGUF quants
  (`--model minimax-maya-xs` / `minimax-maya-s`: [SETTINGS.md](SETTINGS.md#minimax-m31-flash)) are the tested way.
  About 250 GB of VRAM
  and RAM hold all of it; with less the setup warns. The same steps with these differences: the contexts are 32K-1M,
  the recommended one the largest (up to 256K) whose KV cache fits beside the model in this PC's VRAM + RAM, its RAM
  said, with warnings past 256K and when the cache would take the experts' RAM; the pack is `DIR/maya-mm3/`
  (`tools/mm3/pack.py`, Maya's own copy of MiniMax's chat template inside it); pictures are on - the model's vision
  tower runs in the engine (`--no-vision`: off); the config is `maya-mm3.json`, with tensor parallel in groups of 4 or 2
  where the GPUs make whole groups, KV streaming on, MiniMax's sampling (temperature 1.0, top-p 0.95) and thinking level
  Auto; then the DSpark drafter is measured on this PC (`--drafting` chooses without measuring), as DeepSeek's. Its
  settings: [SETTINGS.md](SETTINGS.md#minimax-m31-flash).

GLM-5.3-Flash's first run takes 20-40 minutes plus the download:

1. checks the PC (GPUs, driver, CUDA toolkit, compiler, RAM, CPU);
2. asks which GPUs to use (all of them by default, up to 16), how much context (32K recommended), which model to
   download: GLM-5.3 · Maya-S (recommended; Maya-S24 on cards of 24 GB or less), Maya-M, Maya-M-Derisked or Maya-L, and how it
   drafts: the model's MTP block (the default) or a [DFlash2 drafter](SETTINGS.md#drafting-with-dflash2) (NVIDIA RTX
   30 / A100 and newer);
3. installs its Python packages into `.venv` and gets llama.cpp's source at a pinned commit (it lists both and asks);
4. compiles the engine for your GPU(s) (10-30 minutes, once);
5. **the model**: it shows the source, the size (Maya-S: 96.5 GB) and the exact `curl` commands, and downloads only
   when you answer `y`; every file is checked against its published sha256. You can run the commands yourself
   instead, or use files you already have: `./maya.sh --gguf-dir DIR` - other quants than these four are
   experimental: they run, but Maya is not measured with them. With DFlash2 it also downloads the drafter (0.7 GB,
   checked against its sha256) next to the models;
6. builds the *pack* - the engine's index of the model files, about 1 GB, written into the model folder. A Maya
   model's files that were already there are checked against their sha256 first (once each); the pack records the
   files, and a start refuses it if they change afterwards (`--repack` builds it again);
7. **pictures**: compiles the image encoder (10-20 minutes, once) and fetches its files (1.1 GB, shown and asked
   first); `--no-vision` skips it;
8. writes `maya-<model>.json` and `run-maya-<model>.sh`; with DFlash2 it runs the
   [speed calibration](SETTINGS.md#the-speed-calibration) (5-20 minutes: DFlash2 stays only if it is faster on your
   PC); then the settings [tuned for this PC](SETTINGS.md) earlier, or it offers the tuning (about 10-15 minutes;
   `./maya.sh --calibrate` any time) - and starts the dashboard.

> **The start takes a few minutes**: the engine pins most of the free RAM (all but about 6 GB) for its expert tier
> and warms its caches. Other programs get little RAM while Maya runs.

## Next time, and updating

**Next time**, just run `./setup.sh` again (or the model's `./run-maya-<model>.sh`): it starts right away, nothing is
downloaded twice, on the same screen - its dashboard shows the Monitor's numbers (the speed, where the experts live, the
GPUs, the requests), Tab its log. Ctrl+C stops it - and so does any other way of ending `maya.py`: `kill`, a
service manager, closing the terminal or the window, even a crash: the server and the engine end with it (the
engine keeps its conversation where there is time for that).

**Updating:** the dashboard's **About** says when a new version is out, and its **Update** button does it: Maya
downloads the new code (git), compiles only the engine files that changed and loads the same model again - the model
is not downloaded again, and your settings stay. By hand: `git pull`, then `./setup.sh`.
(The check asks GitHub for the latest release, at most every six hours; `MAYA_UPDATE_CHECK=0` turns it off.)

## Options

| Option | |
| --- | --- |
| `--setup` | set up again (other GPUs, context, model folder) |
| `--check` | only check the PC |
| `--gguf-dir DIR` | use GLM-5.3-Flash GGUF files you already have: their folder, or the `.gguf` file (the first one of a split model) when the folder holds several models. The folder must be writable: the pack goes inside it |
| `--models-dir DIR` | where downloaded models go, each in a folder named after it - `DIR/Maya-L/` holds that model's GGUF files (default `../Maya-data/models`); put it on the NVMe |
| `--download-model` | download the model without asking (the commands and size are still printed) |
| `--model NAME` | which model and files, without asking. Maya's quants by their setup id - the model first, then the tier (XS, S, M, L by the experts' bits): `glm-maya-s`, `glm-maya-s24`, `glm-maya-m`, `glm-maya-m-derisked`, `glm-maya-l`, `deepseek-maya-xs`, `deepseek-maya-s`, `deepseek-maya-m`, `minimax-maya-xs`, `minimax-maya-s` (or the full name, e.g. `MiniMax-M3.1-Flash-Maya-XS`). Or a model, its files asked: `DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017`, `MiniMax-M3.1-Flash`. The names used before still work: `Maya-S-v2-IQ2_XXS` (GLM-5.3 · Maya-S), `Maya-S24`, `Maya-M`, `Maya-M-Derisked`, `Maya-L`, and `EXL3-3.05bpw` / `EXL3-4.05bpw` / `EXL3-sc_4.10bpw` - an experimental EXL3 quant of GLM-5.3-Flash at 3.05 or 4.05 bits per weight, or the self-calibrated one at 4.10 with 8-bit attention and shared experts ([3.05bpw](https://huggingface.co/turboderp/GLM-5.3-Flash-exl3/tree/3.05bpw), 125.3 GB; [4.05bpw](https://huggingface.co/turboderp/GLM-5.3-Flash-exl3/tree/4.05bpw), 165.2 GB; [sc_4.10bpw](https://huggingface.co/turboderp/GLM-5.3-Flash-exl3/tree/sc_4.10bpw), 167.8 GB; all with the MTP block) that the engine reads in place from its safetensors: NVIDIA, and AMD (experimental); 3.05bpw tested on Linux, none measured against the FP8 model |
| `--model-dir DIR` | use DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017's files you already have: the folder with a GGUF's shards, or with the EXL3 quant's files and the Engram shards `model-00047-of-00048.safetensors` / `model-00048-of-00048.safetensors` of DeepSeek's repo (it must be writable: the pack goes inside it) |
| `--ds4-quant Q` | DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017's files: `maya-s`, `maya-xs` (Maya's quants, the same as `--model deepseek-maya-s` / `deepseek-maya-xs`; Maya-S the default), `gguf-q8` (unsloth's UD-Q8_K_XL GGUF), `gguf-q4` (its UD-Q4_K_XL: dense weights Q8_0), `exl3` (LibertAIDAI's EXL3 quant at 2.9 bits) or `exl3-3.5` (its 3.5-bit quant: closer to the original, about 280 GB of VRAM and RAM); the EXL3 quants need NVIDIA V100 or newer (pictures: RTX 30 / A100 or newer) and are offered only there, and on AMD as experimental (as GLM's EXL3 quants there; [docs/EXL3_HIP.md](EXL3_HIP.md)) |
| `--mm3-quant Q` | MiniMax-M3.1-Flash's files: `exl3` (Mia-AiLab's EXL3 quant at 3.5 bits, NVIDIA), `maya-xs` or `maya-s` (Maya's GGUF quants, the same as `--model minimax-maya-xs` / `minimax-maya-s`; `gguf-xs` / `gguf-s` before) |
| `--no-vision` | text only: no image encoder (DeepSeek, MiniMax: their vision tower off) |
| `--draft mtp` / `--draft dflash2` | how the model drafts tokens ahead: its MTP block (the default), or a DFlash2 drafter - downloaded (0.7 GB) into `DIR/GLM-5.3-Flash-DFlash2/` and passed as `--dflash` (Maya-L: +44% on nine GPUs, +12% on two, +19% on one GPU; NVIDIA RTX 30 / A100 and newer - older cards keep the MTP block unless you pass this; the drafter's license is CC BY-NC-ND 4.0, non-commercial use only). The [speed calibration](SETTINGS.md#the-speed-calibration) then keeps it only if it is faster on your PC |
| `--gpu N` / `--gpus 0,1,2,3` | one GPU, or several (up to 16) that split the model's layers |
| `--context N` | context length in tokens: 8192, 32768 (default), 65536, 131072; DeepSeek: 32768, 65536, 131072, 262144, 524288, 1048576 (the default 262144 where VRAM + RAM hold the model, else 65536); MiniMax: the same, the default by this PC's memory |
| `--vram-reserve-mib N` | VRAM in MiB the expert pool leaves free on each GPU (default 700), kept in the config - more where the desktop or other programs on the GPU need room ([the expert pool's size](SETTINGS.md#the-expert-pools-size)) |
| `--port N`, `--host 0.0.0.0 --api-key KEY` | another port; reachable from your network (always set a key; several: `KEY1,KEY2`) |
| `--env KEY=VALUE` | an engine setting kept in the config (see [Tuning](SETTINGS.md)) |
| `--host-compiler g++-12` | when your g++ is newer than your CUDA accepts ("unsupported GNU version") |
| `--rebuild`, `--repack` | compile the engine / build the pack again |
| `--calibrate` | tune the engine for this PC (see [Tuning](SETTINGS.md)): GLM's CPU lane, DeepSeek's `--tp` and CPU threads, MiniMax's [prefill calibration](SETTINGS.md#minimax-m31-flash) (the chunk and the matrix products' library a prompt is read with); then start; with `--no-start` only tune |
| `--speed-calibration` | measure again whether the drafter (DFlash2, or DeepSeek's or MiniMax's DSpark) decodes faster on this PC ([how](SETTINGS.md#the-speed-calibration); 5-30 minutes, with Maya stopped) and keep the faster, then start; with `--no-start` only measure |
| `--drafting measure\|on\|off` | the drafter without the question: `measure` = the speed calibration (the default with `--yes`), `on` = draft with it, `off` = no drafting - both skip the measuring |
| `--no-speed-calibration` | the same as `--drafting on` |
| `--yes` | the recommended answers (the model download still needs `--download-model`) |
| `--plain` | the setup in plain text with typed answers, and Maya in the terminal, not on their own screen (a pipe gets this too, and `--yes` a plain setup); the setup screen's whole output is in `maya-setup.log` |

## Web pages on another address

A browser stops a web page served from another address (a local chat client on `http://127.0.0.1:8090`, say) from
calling Maya's API unless Maya allows that page. List the pages in the config, `maya-<model>.json`:

```json
"cors_origins": ["http://127.0.0.1:8090"]
```

Exact addresses (scheme, host and port, no path); `["*"]` lets every page in - set an `api_key` with it, or any page
you open could use your model. It opens `/v1/*` and nothing else: the settings and the tools stay for Maya's own page.
Without it nothing changes. A new `--setup` writes the config again: add the line back after it.

## Giving the GPU back when idle

To share the PC with other GPU programs (another model server, games), let Maya unload the model after a while without
requests. In the config, `maya-<model>.json`:

```json
"idle_unload_s": 900,
"env": {"STRATA_GLM_SLOT_KEEP": "1"}
```

(`--idle-unload 900` on the server's command line does the same.) After that many seconds with no request running or
waiting, the engine ends: its GPU memory and its pinned RAM tier are given back (an RTX 3090 with Maya-S24: 22.9 GB of
VRAM and 79 GB of RAM). The dashboard and `/health` say `unloaded`, and `/v1/models` still lists the model with the
status `unloaded`. The next request loads the model again - about a minute before its first token - and the experts
in VRAM warm up again over the next answers. With `STRATA_GLM_SLOT_KEEP=1` the conversation in the model is written to
the disk on the way out and read back, so the next turn of the same chat reads only its new tokens (1,630 of 1,690
prompt tokens reused after an unload). `0` (the default): never. A new `--setup` writes the config again: add the
lines back after it.


# Maya on Windows

Back to the [README](../README.md).

Experimental: the same installer sets Maya up natively on Windows 10/11 (64-bit), and the engine and the image
encoder compile there with Visual Studio 2022 and CUDA 12.8 (CUDA 13 works for RTX 20 and newer). Maya is developed
and measured on Linux; on Windows 11 a user runs Maya-L on an RTX 4090, built with CUDA 13.4 (the speed table above,
[#63](https://github.com/mw00/project-maya/issues/63)). Tell us how it runs on yours.

1. Install once:
   - The NVIDIA driver and [CUDA Toolkit 12.8](https://developer.nvidia.com/cuda-12-8-0-download-archive).
     CUDA 13 also works for RTX 20 and newer; V100 needs CUDA 12.x. The CUDA version shown by `nvidia-smi` is the
     driver's capability, not proof that the toolkit (`nvcc`) is installed.
   - [Visual Studio **2022** Build Tools](https://aka.ms/vs/17/release/vs_BuildTools.exe), with
     **Desktop development with C++**, **MSVC v143 - VS 2022 C++ x64/x86 build tools** and a **Windows 10 or 11 SDK**.
     The full Visual Studio IDE is optional. **VS 2026 Build Tools work too with CUDA 13.2 or newer** - which
     Visual Studio a CUDA release supports is in its installation guide ([13.2 onward list VS 2026's MSVC 195x](
     https://docs.nvidia.com/cuda/cuda-installation-guide-microsoft-windows/index.html), 13.1 and every 12.x stop
     at VS 2022); with a CUDA 12.x toolkit, 2026 alone does not satisfy the installer's check;
     2022 can be installed alongside it.
   - **64-bit Python 3.10+**, for example `winget install -e --id Python.Python.3.12 --scope user`.
   - [Git for Windows](https://git-scm.com/download/win) to clone and update the repository, or download and extract
     its source zip instead.
2. Open a **new PowerShell or Command Prompt** after installing the prerequisites. Clone the repository (or open
   the extracted folder), then check the PC:

   ```powershell
   git clone https://github.com/mw00/project-maya.git
   cd project-maya
   .\START-MAYA.bat --check
   ```

   `--check` reports the GPUs, toolkit, compiler, RAM and page file without downloading the model or building the
   engine. Maya finds Visual Studio's compiler environment itself; a Developer Command Prompt is not needed.
3. Start the setup, putting the model on an NVMe SSD with enough [free space](../README.md#what-you-need) for the chosen model.
   Change the drive and folder to suit your PC:

   ```powershell
   .\START-MAYA.bat --setup --models-dir "D:\Maya-models"
   ```

   Choose the model and context when asked. The launcher creates its own `.venv`; setup installs its Python
   packages, CMake and Ninja there, builds the engine, and asks before downloading the model. Later, run
   `.\START-MAYA.bat` (or double-click it) to start the installed model, then open `http://127.0.0.1:8080`.
   All `./maya.sh` options also work here; PowerShell needs the leading `.\`. Add `--plain` for plain-text setup.

- **Check the page-file size, even with "System managed" enabled.** Windows' GPU allocations and Maya's pinned
  RAM tier consume commit capacity (RAM + page file). A small page file can keep the RAM tier below the free RAM
  available, leaving more experts on the SSD. `--check` recommends roughly the selected GPUs' total VRAM plus
  9 GB of page-file capacity (about 57 GB for a 48 GB card). If it warns, set a custom size at least as large as it
  recommends, with enough free disk space: System > About > Advanced system settings > Performance > Settings >
  Advanced > Virtual memory. Restart Windows after changing it. "System managed" can remain too small on a
  large-RAM PC ([#20](https://github.com/mw00/project-maya/issues/20)). On an AMD APU (Strix Halo, Gorgon Halo)
  the page file needs to be at least about the GPU's share of the memory: see
  [AMD_MAYA.md](AMD_MAYA.md#windows) ("The page file on a Windows APU").
- **A GPU filled to the brim pages instead of failing.** Windows' driver moves GPU memory to system RAM when the card
  runs out, and decode then falls to a few tokens per second. The expert pool checks the free VRAM again once it is
  written (`--expert-cache auto`, the default), and the engine log says at start how much is free on each GPU
  (`LOW` below 256 MiB). A drafter (DFlash2) takes a few hundred MB more on its first request. If it warns, or
  answers slow down after the first one, add `"--vram-reserve-mib", "1500"` (say) to the config's `"args"`, or set up
  again with `--vram-reserve-mib 1500` ([the expert pool's size](SETTINGS.md#the-expert-pools-size)).
- With several CUDA toolkits installed, setup chooses the newest compatible one. To select one explicitly, add
  `--nvcc "C:\Program Files\NVIDIA GPU Computing Toolkit\CUDA\v12.8\bin\nvcc.exe"` to the setup command.
- After setup, stop any running Maya server and run `.\START-MAYA.bat --calibrate --no-start` to tune the CPU/PCIe
  split and thread count for your PC. Then start Maya normally. See [Tuning](SETTINGS.md) for the optional RAM-resident
  tier and other settings, and [Something went wrong?](../README.md#something-went-wrong) for `--bench` / `--report`.
- Not WSL2: WSL2's GPU driver pins only about 1 GB of RAM, and Maya pins tens of GB. Run
  `START-MAYA.bat` in Windows itself.
- A Tesla V100 runs it too, built with CUDA 12.4 ([#57](https://github.com/mw00/project-maya/issues/57)). Run
  `--calibrate` once: it tunes Maya for your PC, and a setup for another context keeps its settings.
- AMD on Windows (more experimental still), Strix Halo / Gorgon Halo included: `START-MAYA.bat --backend hip --gpu 0
  --setup` builds the HIP engine with AMD's HIP SDK, or offers AMD's ROCm SDK wheels in `.venv` when there is none
  ([docs/AMD_MAYA.md](AMD_MAYA.md#windows)).
  `tools\hip\build_maya_windows.bat` builds it by hand (an RX 7900 XTX,
  [#54](https://github.com/mw00/project-maya/pull/54)).


# DeepSeek-V4.1 prompt format

The server uses DeepSeek's MIT-licensed `encoding/encoding.py` at revision
`1bae0e1088993b9b82de08f59de5be5a34e42170` of
[deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017](https://huggingface.co/deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017/tree/1bae0e1088993b9b82de08f59de5be5a34e42170/encoding).
The source is vendored as `serve/ds41_encoding.py`, without functional changes;
its copyright and permission notice are in `LICENSE.deepseek-v41`. Other model families keep their Jinja renderer.

The DeepSeek adapter preserves later system/developer messages, reminders, tasks,
message-local tool declarations, tool call/result IDs and text-block boundaries.
Parallel results render in call order. Integer reasoning budgets 1–100 are accepted;
`low`/`high`/`max` mean 50/75/100, and the existing `medium`/`xhigh` aliases mean
75/100. These are prompt conditioning, not completion token limits. Anthropic
`thinking.budget_tokens` still maps to Maya's existing low/high/max levels.

DSML parameter `string` flags determine argument types. Leading answer newlines are
preserved. OpenAI tool responses use the reference's bare `function.name` and optional
call-level `namespace`; internal routing and Anthropic tool names stay qualified
(`namespace::name`). DSML parameters are buffered until their closing tag before
argument JSON is streamed, so the first argument fragment may arrive later.

`tools/ds4/chat_template.jinja` remains a standalone compatibility export for ordinary
chat and tools. It is **not** the server's renderer and does not implement the full
reference protocol: reminders/tasks, message-local tool schemas, later system roles,
ID-based result sorting and namespaces differ. Matching that preprocessing in a
sandboxed Jinja template would duplicate the reference encoder's merge/sort/image and
thinking policies. External consumers needing byte-for-byte conformance should use
the pinned Python encoder and its input format instead. Existing model packs are not
rewritten by this change.

Offline conformance checks (no weights or GPU):

```sh
python -m unittest serve.test_ds41_conformance -v
```

The five official fixture pairs are in `serve/fixtures/deepseek_v41`, with provenance.
The tests also exercise adapter and streaming parser behavior. Image placeholder
conformance does not by itself validate vision inference; malformed/truncated model
output is handled on a best-effort basis rather than claimed as reference-equivalent.
